===============================================================================================
CASE #76: SAN CARLOS MILLING CO., INC. V. COMMISSIONER OF INTERNAL REVENUE
Citation: G.R. No. 103379 | November 23, 1993 | 228 SCRA 135 | Second Division | Ponente: J. Padilla
===============================================================================================

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LAYER A: ALAC DIGEST, RECITATION SCRIPT & STATCON ANALYSIS
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

I. PARTIES & PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND
* Plaintiff/Petitioner: San Carlos Milling Co., Inc. (Petitioner)
* Defendant/Respondent: Commissioner of Internal Revenue and Court of Tax Appeals (Respondents)
* Originating Court: Court of Tax Appeals / Supreme Court
* Nature of the Action: Petition for Review on Certiorari under Rule 45 concerning taxpayer's irrevocable election under Section 69 of the National Internal Revenue Code regarding excess creditable income tax (Tax Refund vs. Tax Credit).

II. WHOLE FACTS
San Carlos Milling had excess creditable withholding taxes. In its final corporate income tax return, it elected to carry over the excess tax as an automatic TAX CREDIT against its estimated income tax liabilities for the succeeding taxable year. However, in the following year, the company suffered net operational losses and incurred no income tax liability. San Carlos Milling then changed its mind and filed an administrative claim with the BIR for a CASH REFUND of the unused excess taxes. The CTA and CA denied the refund, holding that under Section 69 (now Sec. 76) of the NIRC, the disjunctive word 'OR' between refund and tax credit makes the two remedies ALTERNATIVE AND IRREVOCABLE once chosen.

Plain Meaning Summary of Facts:
A company had excess tax payments and ticked the box to carry over the money as an automatic tax credit for next year's taxes. Next year, the company had zero income and owed no taxes. The company then asked the BIR for a cash refund. The BIR and the Supreme Court said NO: the Tax Code says you can choose a refund 'OR' a tax credit. The word 'or' makes the choice mutually exclusive and irrevocable. Once you choose to carry over, you must stick with carrying it over.

III. THE LEGAL ISSUE
Whether or not a taxpayer who has chosen to carry over excess creditable taxes as a tax credit may subsequently change its election and claim a cash refund, testing the Disjunctive Particle 'OR' in Section 69 NIRC.

[HOW TO TACKLE IN RECITATION: THE LEGAL ISSUE]
* Plain Meaning: Does the word 'OR' in the tax refund law make the choice between a cash refund and carrying over tax credits mutually exclusive and irrevocable?
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the issue is whether the disjunctive word 'OR' in Section 69 of the NIRC establishes alternative and mutually exclusive remedies, testing the Construction of Disjunctive Particles ('Or' vs. 'And') and the Irrevocability Rule in Tax Law."

IV. ALAC DIGEST

[A] ANSWER:
NO. The taxpayer CANNOT switch to a cash refund. In statutory construction, the word 'OR' is a DISJUNCTIVE CONJUNCTION that indicates an alternative; the two remedies are MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE, and once an election is made, it is IRREVOCABLE.

* Plain Meaning: No. The word 'or' separates two different choices: you can take a refund, OR carry it over. You cannot pick both, and once you pick carry-over, the choice is locked permanently until consumed. 'Or' means alternative.
* Recitation Script: "No, Sir/Ma'am. The Supreme Court held that the word 'or' is a disjunctive particle expressing an alternative. Under Section 69 of the NIRC, a corporate taxpayer is given the option either to obtain a refund OR to carry over the excess credit. Once the taxpayer elects to carry over, the two remedies are mutually exclusive, and the choice is irrevocable under the explicit statutory text."

[L] LEGAL BASIS:
* 1. Section 69 (now Section 76), National Internal Revenue Code: Provides that a corporation with excess quarterly taxes may either (a) be refunded, OR (b) automatically credit the excess against future taxes.
* 2. Construction of Disjunctive Particle 'OR': The word 'or' connects mutually exclusive alternatives, signifying that one or the other, but not both, may be chosen.
* 3. Strictissimi Juris (of the strictest interpretation) in Tax Refunds: Tax refund rules are strictly construed against the taxpayer; procedural statutory conditions are mandatory.
* Plain Meaning: Section 69 NIRC, Disjunctive Particle 'Or', and Strictissimi Juris in tax refunds.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the legal basis is Section 69 of the NIRC, governed by the grammatical and statutory rule on the Disjunctive Particle 'OR' and Strictissimi Juris in tax refunds."

[A] ANALYSIS / APPLICATION:
* 1. Disjunctive Nature of 'Or': In statutory construction, 'or' is a disjunctive that marks an alternative which generally corresponds to the word 'either' ('either this OR that'). It separates distinct items and establishes mutual exclusivity.
* 2. The Irrevocability Rule: Allowing taxpayers to switch back and forth between tax credits and cash refunds would create administrative chaos in the BIR's revenue forecasting and auditing systems.
* 3. Protection of Unused Credits: While the taxpayer cannot get a cash refund, its excess credit is not lost; it remains carried over indefinitely until completely absorbed against future corporate tax liabilities.
* Plain Meaning: In English and statutory construction, 'OR' means you choose Path A or Path B, not both. The law was written this way so the BIR knows whether to set aside cash for a check or just adjust future bookkeeping. Once you tick 'carry over', the tax code locks your choice.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the Court applied the disjunctive rule for the word 'or'. 'Or' connects alternative, mutually exclusive choices. Under Section 69 NIRC, electing to carry over excess credits permanently bars a subsequent claim for cash refund, enforcing administrative order in taxation."

[C] CONCLUSION:
The Supreme Court DENIED the petition and AFFIRMED the decision of the Court of Tax Appeals denying the cash refund.

* Plain Meaning: The cash refund was denied and the taxpayer was held to its choice to carry over the credits.
* Recitation Script: "The Supreme Court affirmed the denial of the refund, ruling that the disjunctive 'or' establishes mutually exclusive and irrevocable tax remedies."

V. RELATION TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Syllabus Topic Alignment:
  • Chapter V: Interpretation of Words and Phrases
  - Disjunctive vs. Conjunctive Words: The Meaning and Effect of 'Or' in Tax and Procedural Statutes

* Governing Latin Maxims with English Translations:
  1. Verba legis non est recedendum (from the words of the statute there should be no departure)
     - Literal Translation: From the words of the statute there must be no departure.
     - StatCon Application: The disjunctive particle 'or' must be given its plain alternative meaning.
  2. Strictissimi juris (of the strictest interpretation)
     - Literal Translation: Of the strictest law.
     - StatCon Application: Tax refund statutes are construed strictissimi juris against the claimant.
  3. Electa una via, non datur recursus ad alteram (having chosen one way, recourse to another is not given)
     - Literal Translation: When one way is chosen, recourse to another is barred.
     - StatCon Application: Choosing the carry-over option irrevocably bars the cash refund option.

* Case Violation in StatCon: The taxpayer attempted to treat the disjunctive particle 'or' as conjunctive ('and'), switching between mutually exclusive statutory options.
* Doctrinal Execution: The Disjunctive Particle 'Or': The word 'or' is a disjunctive conjunction that connects separate, mutually exclusive alternatives; under the doctrine Electa Una Via, selecting one alternative irrevocably forecloses the other.

* Plain StatCon Meaning: The word 'OR' means you pick one option and stick with it; you cannot choose option A and then try to take option B.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, San Carlos Milling v. CIR is the leading authority on the disjunctive particle 'OR' in statutory construction: 'or' establishes alternative, mutually exclusive, and irrevocable remedies."

LAYER B: STANDARDIZED CASE DIGEST (STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION FORMAT)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Case Title: San Carlos Milling Co., Inc. v. Commissioner of Internal Revenue
G.R. No.:   G.R. No. 103379
Date:       November 23, 1993
Ponente:    Second Division

I. FACTS OF THE CASE
San Carlos Milling had excess creditable withholding taxes. In its final corporate income tax return, it elected to carry over the excess tax as an automatic TAX CREDIT against its estimated income tax liabilities for the succeeding taxable year. However, in the following year, the company suffered net operational losses and incurred no income tax liability. The CTA and CA denied the refund, holding that under Section 69 (now Sec. 76) of the NIRC, the disjunctive word 'OR' between refund and tax credit makes the two remedies ALTERNATIVE AND IRREVOCABLE once chosen.

II. FACTS RELEVANT TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Provision Relevant: 1. Section 69 (now Section 76), National Internal Revenue Code: Provides that a corporation with excess quarterly taxes may either (a) be refunded, OR (b) automatically credit the excess against future taxes.
2. Construction of Disjunctive Particle 'OR': The word 'or' connects mutually exclusive alternatives, signifying that one or the other, but not both, may be chosen.
* Circumstance Making Construction Necessary: The taxpayer attempted to treat the disjunctive particle 'or' as conjunctive ('and'), switching between mutually exclusive statutory options.
* Competing Interpretations: Petitioner argued for a favorable interpretation based on statutory intent, while Respondent contended for a literal or restrictive application.

III. ISSUES
A. General Legal Issues: Whether or not a taxpayer who has chosen to carry over excess creditable taxes as a tax credit may subsequently change its election and claim a cash refund, testing the Disjunctive Particle 'OR' in Section 69 NIRC.
B. Commissioner of Internal Revenue, under the canon of Electa una via, non datur recursus ad alteram.

IV. PROVISION/S OF LAW CONSTRUED
* Provision: 1. Section 69 (now Section 76), National Internal Revenue Code: Provides that a corporation with excess quarterly taxes may either (a) be refunded, OR (b) automatically credit the excess against future taxes.
2. Construction of Disjunctive Particle 'OR': The word 'or' connects mutually exclusive alternatives, signifying that one or the other, but not both, may be chosen.
* Interpretive Problem: Determining the precise legislative intent and boundary of the statutory terms in light of: The taxpayer attempted to treat the disjunctive particle 'or' as conjunctive ('and'), switching between mutually exclusi...

V. RULING OF THE SUPREME COURT
A. General Ruling: The Supreme Court DENIED the petition and AFFIRMED the decision of the Court of Tax Appeals denying the cash refund.
B. Ruling on Statutory-Construction Issue: NO. The taxpayer CANNOT switch to a cash refund. In statutory construction, the word 'OR' is a DISJUNCTIVE CONJUNCTION that indicates an alternative; the two remedies are MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE, and once an election is made, it is IRREVOCABLE.

VI. HOW THE COURT CONSTRUED THE LAW
A. Why Was Construction Necessary? Ambiguity in the application and scope of the statute: The taxpayer attempted to treat the disjunctive particle 'or' as conjunctive ('and'), switching between mutually exclusive statutory options.
B. Purpose of Construction: To ascertain and give full effect to the true intent, spirit, and purpose of the lawmaking body.
C. Method/Canon Applied: Electa una via, non datur recursus ad alteram
   - How Applied: The Disjunctive Particle 'Or': The word 'or' is a disjunctive conjunction that connects separate, mutually exclusive alternatives; under the doctrine Electa Una Via, selecting one alternative irrevocably forecloses the other.
   - Result: No. The word 'or' separates two different choices: you can take a refund, OR carry it over. You cannot pick both, and once you pick carry-over, the choice is locked permanently until consumed. 'Or' means alternative.
D. Other Sources Used: Ordinary meaning of words, statute considered as a whole, legislative history, and established Supreme Court jurisprudence.

VII. RELEVANT STATUTORY-CONSTRUCTION CONCEPTS (A–L EVALUATION)
* A. Situs of Statutory Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Supreme Court of the Philippines, exercising its constitutional judicial power under Article VIII of the Constitution, serves as the ultimate situs and final arbiter for interpreting and construing the disputed provision.
* B. Purpose of Statutory Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The purpose of construction is to discover and effectuate the true legislative intent and will of the lawmaking body, preventing injustice and ensuring the statute operates harmoniously.
* C. When to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court reaffirmed the cardinal threshold rule: Construction and interpretation are proper only where the statute is ambiguous, doubtful, or susceptible of multiple meanings. Where the law is clear, no construction is warranted.
* D. Ambiguity or Vagueness - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The statutory language was clear and unambiguous; the Court declined to find ambiguity where none existed in the text.
* E. Verba Legis Non Est Recedendum - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court adhered strictly to the literal wording of the statute, holding that from the words of a statute there should be no departure.
* F. Absoluta Sententia Expositore Non Indigent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court held that when the language of the law is clear and unequivocal, it requires no interpreter and leaves no room for judicial construction.
* G. Legislative Intent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court analyzed the true intent and policy of the lawmaking authority, ensuring that the statutory purpose is preserved and not defeated by erroneous administrative or private interpretations.
* H. Strict or Liberal Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court applied the recognized standard of construction tailored to the nature of the statute (e.g., penal and tax statutes are strictly construed against the State, whereas social legislation and remedial rules are liberally construed).
* I. Prospective or Retrospective Construction - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The case involved only the contemporaneous, prospective application of the governing law.
* J. Statutory Construction vs. Judicial Legislation - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court underscored the constitutional boundary of judicial power: Courts are empowered solely to interpret (jus dicere), not to enact or amend laws (jus dare). Supplying unwritten terms or expanding clear prohibitions constitutes prohibited judicial legislation.
* K. Legis Interpretatio Legis Vim Obtinet - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. Under Article 8 of the Civil Code, the Supreme Court's authoritative interpretation of the statute forms part of the legal system of the Philippines and acquires the force of law (*legis interpretatio legis vim obtinet*).
* L. Extent and Limits of the Power to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court respected the constitutional limits of its authority by applying the law as written and refusing to invade the legislative domain under the guise of equity or expediency.

VIII. OTHER CANONS OR METHODS OF CONSTRUCTION

• Strictissimi juris (strict construction of refund provisions)

* Effect on Interpretation: Harmonized the statute and prevented an absurd or unjust result.

Commissioner of Internal Revenue to mean and apply strictly in accordance with Electa una via, non datur recursus ad alteram, holding that No. The word 'or' separates two different choices: you can take a refund, OR carry it over. You cannot pick both, and once you pick carry-over, the choice is lo.

===============================================================================================
CASE #77: ZACARIAS BERCES, SR. V. HON. TEOFISTO T. GUINGONA, JR. AND MAYOR NAOMI CORRAL
Citation: G.R. No. 112099 | February 21, 1995 | 241 SCRA 539 | En Banc | Ponente: J. Quiason
===============================================================================================

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LAYER A: ALAC DIGEST, RECITATION SCRIPT & STATCON ANALYSIS
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

I. PARTIES & PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND
* Plaintiff/Petitioner: Zacarias Berces, Sr. (Petitioner)
* Defendant/Respondent: Hon. Teofisto T. Guingona, Jr., Executive Secretary, and Mayor Naomi Corral (Respondents)
* Originating Court: Office of the President / Supreme Court En Banc
* Nature of the Action: Petition for Certiorari under Rule 65 concerning whether an appeal to the Office of the President from a Sangguniang Panlalawigan disciplinary order automatically stays execution under Section 68 of Republic Act No. 7160 (Local Government Code).

II. WHOLE FACTS
Zacarias Berces filed administrative complaints against Tiwi, Albay Municipal Mayor Naomi Corral before the Sangguniang Panlalawigan of Albay. The Sanggunian found the Mayor guilty of abuse of authority and suspended her for two consecutive terms of 60 days. Mayor Corral appealed the suspension to the Office of the President pursuant to Section 67 of the Local Government Code, and the Executive Secretary issued a Stay of Execution. Berces challenged the stay order, contending that under Section 68 of the LGC, execution of the Sanggunian's suspension order is mandatory, and arguing that the word 'may' in Section 68 should be read as mandatory upon the local sanggunian.

Plain Meaning Summary of Facts:
A provincial board suspended a town mayor for 60 days. The mayor appealed to the President of the Philippines, and the Executive Secretary stayed (paused) the suspension while the appeal was being studied. The complainant sued, arguing that under Section 68 of the Local Government Code, the suspension must be carried out immediately and the President had no power to pause it. The Supreme Court ruled that Section 68 uses the permissive word 'may', granting the Office of the President full discretionary power to stay execution during an appeal.

III. THE LEGAL ISSUE
Whether or not the word 'may' in Section 68 of the Local Government Code is permissive and authorizes the Office of the President to stay the execution of an administrative suspension pending appeal.

[HOW TO TACKLE IN RECITATION: THE LEGAL ISSUE]
* Plain Meaning: Does the word 'may' give the President discretionary power to pause a mayor's suspension while the appeal is pending?
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the issue is whether the word 'may' in Section 68 of RA 7160 confers discretionary power to stay execution on appeal, testing the Construction of Permissive Words ('May') and Executive Supervision."

IV. ALAC DIGEST

[A] ANSWER:
YES. The word 'may' is PERMISSIVE AND DISCRETIONARY. Section 68 of the Local Government Code explicitly empowers the Office of the President, in its sound discretion, to stay the execution of a preventive or disciplinary suspension pending appeal.

* Plain Meaning: Yes. The statute says the appeal 'may' stay execution. 'May' gives the President the legal option to pause the suspension so the mayor is not unfairly removed before the President finishes reviewing the case.
* Recitation Script: "Yes, Sir/Ma'am. The Supreme Court held that under statutory construction, 'may' is an auxiliary verb indicating discretion and permission. Under Section 68 of RA 7160, an appeal to the Office of the President does not automatically stay execution, but the President possesses the discretionary authority under 'may' to grant a stay of execution where justice requires."

[L] LEGAL BASIS:
* 1. Section 68, Republic Act No. 7160 (Local Government Code of 1991): Provides that an appeal shall not prevent the execution of a decision, but the Office of the President 'may' grant a stay.
* 2. Construction of 'May' as Permissive: The word 'may' is prima facie directory and confers discretionary authority upon appellate bodies.
* 3. Article X, Section 4, 1987 Constitution: General supervisory power of the President over local government units.
* Plain Meaning: Section 68 RA 7160, Construction of 'May' as Permissive, and Constitutional Presidential Supervision.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the legal basis is Section 68 of RA 7160 in relation to Article X, Section 4 of the Constitution, applied under the rule that 'may' confers discretionary authority."

[A] ANALYSIS / APPLICATION:
* 1. Permissive Meaning of 'May': The statutory phrasing 'may be stayed by the Office of the President' plainly leaves the decision to the sound judgment of the President. It is neither an automatic stay nor a total prohibition against stays.
* 2. Presidential Power of General Supervision: The President's constitutional duty to ensure that laws are faithfully executed includes the administrative prerogative to maintain the status quo and prevent political disruption while reviewing local disciplinary orders.
* 3. Harmonization of Local Autonomy and Executive Oversight: Section 68 strikes a balance: the local decision is executable, but the President can intervene to prevent irreparable injury to local governance.
* Plain Meaning: If the law meant the suspension could NEVER be paused, it would have said 'shall not be stayed'. Instead, Congress wrote 'may be stayed by the Office of the President'. That one word 'may' gives the President the right to freeze the suspension if the appeal looks strong.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the Court applied the permissive construction of 'may'. 'May' indicates discretion. Section 68 RA 7160 grants the Office of the President the discretionary power to stay execution to prevent administrative disruption while exercising constitutional supervision."

[C] CONCLUSION:
The Supreme Court DISMISSED Berces' petition and UPHELD the Stay of Execution issued by the Office of the President.

* Plain Meaning: The petition was dismissed and the stay order issued by the President was affirmed.
* Recitation Script: "The Supreme Court upheld the stay of execution, confirming that 'may' in Section 68 LGC is permissive and discretionary."

V. RELATION TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Syllabus Topic Alignment:
  • Chapter V: Interpretation of Words and Phrases
  - Permissive Words ('May') in Administrative Appeals & Presidential General Supervision

* Governing Latin Maxims with English Translations:
  1. Verba legis non est recedendum (from the words of the statute there should be no departure)
     - Literal Translation: From the words of the statute there must be no departure.
     - StatCon Application: The permissive word 'may' must be given its ordinary discretionary meaning.
  2. Potestas delegata non delegari potest (a delegated power cannot be delegated)
     - Literal Translation: Delegated authority is exercised within statutory boundaries.
     - StatCon Application: The President exercises statutory discretionary power under Section 68 LGC.
  3. Salus populi est suprema lex (the welfare of the people is the supreme law)
     - Literal Translation: The welfare of the people is the supreme law.
     - StatCon Application: Preventing political disruption in local government units serves the public order.

* Case Violation in StatCon: The petitioner attempted to rewrite a permissive statutory term ('may') into an absolute prohibition against presidential stay orders.
* Doctrinal Execution: Discretionary Nature of 'May' in Appellate Review: In statutory provisions governing appeals, the term 'may' confers a broad, sound discretion upon the appellate authority to grant interim stays of execution.

* Plain StatCon Meaning: When a law says an appellate body 'MAY' stay an order, it gives the reviewer total discretion to pause the penalty while studying the case.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, Berces v. Guingona confirms that the word 'may' in Section 68 of the Local Government Code confers discretionary power on the President to stay administrative executions."

LAYER B: STANDARDIZED CASE DIGEST (STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION FORMAT)

Case Title: Zacarias Berces, Sr. v. Hon. Teofisto T. Guingona, Jr. and Mayor Naomi Corral

C. Method/Canon Applied: Verba legis non est recedendum
   - How Applied: Discretionary Nature of 'May' in Appellate Review: In statutory provisions governing appeals, the term 'may' confers a broad, sound discretion upon the appellate authority to grant interim stays of execution.

VII. RELEVANT STATUTORY-CONSTRUCTION CONCEPTS (A–L EVALUATION)

* F. Absoluta Sententia Expositore Non Indigent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court held that when the language of the law is clear and unequivocal, it requires no interpreter and leaves no room for judicial construction.
* G. Legislative Intent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court analyzed the true intent and policy of the lawmaking authority, ensuring that the statutory purpose is preserved and not defeated by erroneous administrative or private interpretations.
* H. Strict or Liberal Construction - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The Court applied standard ordinary rules of statutory interpretation without resorting to special strict or liberal construction doctrines.
* I. Prospective or Retrospective Construction - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The case involved only the contemporaneous, prospective application of the governing law.
* J. Statutory Construction vs. Judicial Legislation - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court underscored the constitutional boundary of judicial power: Courts are empowered solely to interpret (jus dicere), not to enact or amend laws (jus dare). Supplying unwritten terms or expanding clear prohibitions constitutes prohibited judicial legislation.
* K. Legis Interpretatio Legis Vim Obtinet - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. Under Article 8 of the Civil Code, the Supreme Court's authoritative interpretation of the statute forms part of the legal system of the Philippines and acquires the force of law (*legis interpretatio legis vim obtinet*).
* L. Extent and Limits of the Power to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court respected the constitutional limits of its authority by applying the law as written and refusing to invade the legislative domain under the guise of equity or expediency.

VIII. OTHER CANONS OR METHODS OF CONSTRUCTION
* Canon/Method: • Potestas delegata (statutory supervisory powers)
• Salus populi est suprema lex (stability of local governance)
* How Applied: Applied as corroborative canons to reinforce the primary rule of construction.
* Effect on Interpretation: Harmonized the statute and prevented an absurd or unjust result.

IX. THE CONSTRUCTION MADE BY THE COURT
* Summary Formula: The Court construed the disputed statutory provisions in Zacarias Berces, Sr. v. Hon. Teofisto T. Guingona, Jr. and Mayor Naomi Corral to mean and apply strictly in accordance with Verba legis non est recedendum, holding that Yes. The statute says the appeal 'may' stay execution. 'May' gives the President the legal option to pause the suspension so the mayor is not unfairly removed b.
* Principal Reasoning: If the law meant the suspension could NEVER be paused, it would have said 'shall not be stayed'. Instead, Congress wrote 'may be stayed by the Office of the President'. That one word 'may' gives the President the right to freeze the suspension if the appeal looks strong.

X. STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION OR JUDICIAL LEGISLATION?
[X] Proper statutory construction    [ ] Judicial legislation    [ ] Debatable
Explanation: The Supreme Court acted within the legitimate bounds of its constitutional power to interpret the law (jus dicere). By applying Verba legis non est recedendum, the Court faithfully effectuated the legislative intent without creating new rules, modifying clear statutory text, or usurping congressional prerogative. The ruling reinforces the separation of powers and the doctrine that courts must enforce the law as enacted.

XI. DOCTRINE:
Discretionary Nature of 'May' in Appellate Review: In statutory provisions governing appeals, the term 'may' confers a broad, sound discretion upon the appellate authority to grant interim stays of execution.

XII. ONE-SENTENCE TAKEAWAY:
This case is important in Statutory Construction because When a law says an appellate body 'MAY' stay an order, it gives the reviewer total discretion to pause the penalty while studying the case.

===============================================================================================
CASE #78: CHANG YUNG FA AND CO KIM CHAM V. HON. EMILIO L. GIANZO
Citation: G.R. No. L-7885 | November 25, 1955 | 97 Phil. 913 | En Banc | Ponente: J. Montemayor
===============================================================================================

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LAYER A: ALAC DIGEST, RECITATION SCRIPT & STATCON ANALYSIS
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

I. PARTIES & PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND
* Plaintiff/Petitioner: Chang Yung Fa and Co Kim Cham (Petitioners)
* Defendant/Respondent: Hon. Emilio L. Gianzo, Commissioner of Immigration (Respondent)
* Originating Court: Supreme Court En Banc (Original Action for Mandamus and Prohibition)
* Nature of the Action: Petition for Mandamus and Prohibition challenging the authority of the Commissioner of Immigration to deport temporary Chinese visitors upon the expiration of their authorized stay under Commonwealth Act No. 613 (The Philippine Immigration Act of 1940).

II. WHOLE FACTS
Petitioners, Chinese citizens admitted to the Philippines as temporary visitors under Commonwealth Act No. 613, sought indefinite extensions of stay due to political turmoil in mainland China. The Commissioner of Immigration denied further extensions and issued warrants of deportation pursuant to Section 37 of CA 613, which provides that any alien who remains in the Philippines beyond the period permitted 'SHALL BE DEPORTED' by order of the Commissioner. Petitioners filed for Mandamus, arguing that Section 47 of the law says the President or Commissioner 'MAY extend' visits, and claimed that 'shall be deported' should be construed flexibly and directories in humanitarian cases.

Plain Meaning Summary of Facts:
Temporary foreign visitors from China whose visas expired demanded that they be allowed to stay indefinitely because of political conflict in their home country. The Immigration Commissioner ordered them deported under the law which says illegal overstaying aliens 'shall be deported'. The foreigners argued that 'shall' should be read as flexible and optional. The Supreme Court ruled that 'shall be deported' is strictly mandatory, and once an alien's visa expires, the Commissioner is required by law to deport them.

III. THE LEGAL ISSUE
Whether or not the phrase 'shall be deported' in Section 37 of the Philippine Immigration Act of 1940 is mandatory, leaving the Immigration Commissioner with no discretion to allow overstaying aliens to remain.

[HOW TO TACKLE IN RECITATION: THE LEGAL ISSUE]
* Plain Meaning: Is the phrase 'shall be deported' mandatory when an alien overstays his visa?
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the issue is whether the phrase 'shall be deported' in Section 37 of CA 613 is mandatory, testing the Construction of Mandatory Words ('Shall') in immigration and deportation law."

IV. ALAC DIGEST

[A] ANSWER:
YES. The phrase 'shall be deported' is STRICTLY MANDATORY. Once an alien overstays his authorized visa, the Commissioner of Immigration has an imperative, ministerial duty to arrest and deport the alien.

* Plain Meaning: Yes. The law commands that an overstaying foreigner 'shall be deported'. An alien has no constitutional right to remain in a foreign country once his visa expires. 'Shall' means the government must enforce deportation.
* Recitation Script: "Yes, Sir/Ma'am. The Supreme Court held that under Verba Legis and the mandatory force of 'shall', Section 37 of CA 613 imposes an imperative duty on the Commissioner of Immigration to execute deportation against overstaying temporary visitors. National sovereignty dictates that alien stays are privileges strictly governed by statute."

[L] LEGAL BASIS:
* 1. Section 37(a)(7), Commonwealth Act No. 613 (Philippine Immigration Act of 1940): Mandates that any alien who remains in the Philippines in violation of any limitation or condition 'shall be arrested and deported'.
* 2. Mandatory Construction of 'Shall': The word 'shall' is a word of command leaving no administrative discretion to withhold execution.
* 3. Inherent Sovereign Power of Expulsion: The State has the plenary sovereign right to expel aliens who violate immigration terms.
* Plain Meaning: Section 37 CA 613, Mandatory 'Shall', and Sovereign Power of Deportation.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the legal basis is Section 37 of CA 613, applied under the mandatory construction of 'shall' and the inherent sovereign prerogative of alien expulsion."

[A] ANALYSIS / APPLICATION:
* 1. Mandatory Force of 'Shall': The statutory directive 'shall be deported' is absolute and imperative. The Commissioner of Immigration has no discretion to convert temporary visitors into permanent residents or allow indefinite overstays.
* 2. Absence of Vested Right: A temporary visitor enters the country under a conditional privilege with an explicit promise to leave upon expiration of the authorized period. No vested right is acquired.
* 3. Subordination of Humanitarian Excuses: Political instability abroad does not suspend the operation of domestic immigration statutes. Changes in immigration policy belong exclusively to the political branches.
* Plain Meaning: When you enter a country on a tourist visa, you promise to leave when your time is up. The statute says overstaying aliens 'shall be deported'. The word 'shall' gives the Immigration Commissioner a direct order to send you home. Judges cannot turn 'shall' into a permanent stay permit.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the Court applied the mandatory force of 'shall'. Section 37 of CA 613 leaves zero discretion. Temporary visitors who overstay are subject to mandatory deportation under the plain text of the Immigration Act."

[C] CONCLUSION:
The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition for Mandamus and Prohibition and UPHELD the deportation orders issued by the Commissioner of Immigration.

* Plain Meaning: The petition was dismissed and the deportation of the overstaying aliens was affirmed.
* Recitation Script: "The Supreme Court dismissed the petition, ruling that 'shall be deported' in CA 613 is mandatory under Verba Legis."

V. RELATION TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Syllabus Topic Alignment:
  • Chapter V: Interpretation of Words and Phrases
  - Mandatory Words: The Imperative Force of 'Shall' in Immigration and Deportation Statutes

* Governing Latin Maxims with English Translations:
  1. Verba legis non est recedendum (from the words of the statute there should be no departure)
     - Literal Translation: From the words of the statute there must be no departure.
     - StatCon Application: The command 'shall be deported' in CA 613 must be enforced as enacted.
  2. Dura lex, sed lex (the law is harsh, but it is the law)
     - Literal Translation: The law is harsh, but it is the law.
     - StatCon Application: Deportation of overstaying aliens is a harsh but mandatory consequence of immigration laws.
  3. Salus populi est suprema lex (the welfare of the people is the supreme law)
     - Literal Translation: The welfare of the people is the supreme law.
     - StatCon Application: Control of national borders and expulsion of unauthorized aliens preserves national sovereignty.

* Case Violation in StatCon: The petitioners attempted to convert a mandatory statutory command ('shall be deported') into a discretionary option to secure indefinite residency.
* Doctrinal Execution: Mandatory Character of Immigration Expulsion: Where an immigration statute provides that unauthorized aliens 'shall be deported', the statutory command is imperative, non-discretionary, and enforceable by the executive.

* Plain StatCon Meaning: When the immigration law says overstaying foreigners 'SHALL BE DEPORTED', it is a direct order that must be enforced.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, Chang Yung Fa v. Gianzo confirms that 'shall be deported' in Commonwealth Act No. 613 is strictly mandatory under Verba Legis."

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LAYER B: STANDARDIZED CASE DIGEST (STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION FORMAT)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Case Title: Chang Yung Fa and Co Kim Cham v. Hon. Emilio L. Gianzo
G.R. No.:   G.R. No. L-7885
Date:       November 25, 1955
Ponente:    En Banc

I. FACTS OF THE CASE
Petitioners, Chinese citizens admitted to the Philippines as temporary visitors under Commonwealth Act No. 613, sought indefinite extensions of stay due to political turmoil in mainland China. The Commissioner of Immigration denied further extensions and issued warrants of deportation pursuant to Section 37 of CA 613, which provides that any alien who remains in the Philippines beyond the period permitted 'SHALL BE DEPORTED' by order of the Commissioner. Petitioners filed for Mandamus, arguing that Section 47 of the law says the President or Commissioner 'MAY extend' visits, and claimed that 'shall be deported' should be construed flexibly and directories in humanitarian cases.

II. FACTS RELEVANT TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Provision Relevant: 1. Section 37(a)(7), Commonwealth Act No. 613 (Philippine Immigration Act of 1940): Mandates that any alien who remains in the Philippines in violation of any limitation or condition 'shall be arrested and deported'.
2. Mandatory Construction of 'Shall': The word 'shall' is a word of command leaving no administrative discretion to withhold execution.
* Circumstance Making Construction Necessary: The petitioners attempted to convert a mandatory statutory command ('shall be deported') into a discretionary option to secure indefinite residency.
* Competing Interpretations: Petitioner argued for a favorable interpretation based on statutory intent, while Respondent contended for a literal or restrictive application.

III. ISSUES
A. General Legal Issues: Whether or not the phrase 'shall be deported' in Section 37 of the Philippine Immigration Act of 1940 is mandatory, leaving the Immigration Commissioner with no discretion to allow overstaying aliens to remain.
B. Statutory-Construction Issue/s: Whether the subject statutory act, classification, or terminology under the governing statute/rule means, includes, or applies to the factual controversy in Chang Yung Fa and Co Kim Cham v. Hon. Emilio L. Gianzo, under the canon of Verba legis non est recedendum.

IV. PROVISION/S OF LAW CONSTRUED
* Provision: 1. Section 37(a)(7), Commonwealth Act No. 613 (Philippine Immigration Act of 1940): Mandates that any alien who remains in the Philippines in violation of any limitation or condition 'shall be arrested and deported'.
2. Mandatory Construction of 'Shall': The word 'shall' is a word of command leaving no administrative discretion to withhold execution.
* Interpretive Problem: The text of the statute is plain and unambiguous, raising the question of whether courts may depart from its literal wording under the guise of interpretation.

V. RULING OF THE SUPREME COURT
A. General Ruling: The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition for Mandamus and Prohibition and UPHELD the deportation orders issued by the Commissioner of Immigration.
B. Ruling on Statutory-Construction Issue: YES. The phrase 'shall be deported' is STRICTLY MANDATORY. Once an alien overstays his authorized visa, the Commissioner of Immigration has an imperative, ministerial duty to arrest and deport the alien.

VI. HOW THE COURT CONSTRUED THE LAW
A. Why Was Construction Necessary? A party sought to create an exception or import non-textual requirements into a clear statute; the Court intervened to reassert that plain language admits of no judicial construction.
B. Purpose of Construction: To uphold the plain meaning of the statute, preserve legislative supremacy, and avoid arbitrary judicial expansion.
C. Method/Canon Applied: Verba legis non est recedendum
   - How Applied: Mandatory Character of Immigration Expulsion: Where an immigration statute provides that unauthorized aliens 'shall be deported', the statutory command is imperative, non-discretionary, and enforceable by the executive.
   - Result: Yes. The law commands that an overstaying foreigner 'shall be deported'. An alien has no constitutional right to remain in a foreign country once his visa expires. 'Shall' means the government must enforce deportation.
D. Other Sources Used: Ordinary meaning of words, statute considered as a whole, legislative history, and established Supreme Court jurisprudence.

VII. RELEVANT STATUTORY-CONSTRUCTION CONCEPTS (A–L EVALUATION)
* A. Situs of Statutory Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Supreme Court of the Philippines, exercising its constitutional judicial power under Article VIII of the Constitution, serves as the ultimate situs and final arbiter for interpreting and construing the disputed provision.
* B. Purpose of Statutory Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The purpose of construction is to discover and effectuate the true legislative intent and will of the lawmaking body, preventing injustice and ensuring the statute operates harmoniously.
* C. When to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court reaffirmed the cardinal threshold rule: Construction and interpretation are proper only where the statute is ambiguous, doubtful, or susceptible of multiple meanings. Where the law is clear, no construction is warranted.
* D. Ambiguity or Vagueness - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The statutory language was clear and unambiguous; the Court declined to find ambiguity where none existed in the text.
* E. Verba Legis Non Est Recedendum - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court adhered strictly to the literal wording of the statute, holding that from the words of a statute there should be no departure.
* F. Absoluta Sententia Expositore Non Indigent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court held that when the language of the law is clear and unequivocal, it requires no interpreter and leaves no room for judicial construction.
* G. Legislative Intent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court analyzed the true intent and policy of the lawmaking authority, ensuring that the statutory purpose is preserved and not defeated by erroneous administrative or private interpretations.
* H. Strict or Liberal Construction - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The Court applied standard ordinary rules of statutory interpretation without resorting to special strict or liberal construction doctrines.
* I. Prospective or Retrospective Construction - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The case involved only the contemporaneous, prospective application of the governing law.
* J. Statutory Construction vs. Judicial Legislation - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court underscored the constitutional boundary of judicial power: Courts are empowered solely to interpret (jus dicere), not to enact or amend laws (jus dare). Supplying unwritten terms or expanding clear prohibitions constitutes prohibited judicial legislation.
* K. Legis Interpretatio Legis Vim Obtinet - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. Under Article 8 of the Civil Code, the Supreme Court's authoritative interpretation of the statute forms part of the legal system of the Philippines and acquires the force of law (*legis interpretatio legis vim obtinet*).
* L. Extent and Limits of the Power to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court respected the constitutional limits of its authority by applying the law as written and refusing to invade the legislative domain under the guise of equity or expediency.

VIII. OTHER CANONS OR METHODS OF CONSTRUCTION
* Canon/Method: • Dura lex sed lex (harsh consequence of overstaying)
• Salus populi est suprema lex (border sovereignty)
* How Applied: Applied as corroborative canons to reinforce the primary rule of construction.
* Effect on Interpretation: Harmonized the statute and prevented an absurd or unjust result.

IX. THE CONSTRUCTION MADE BY THE COURT
* Summary Formula: The Court construed the disputed statutory provisions in Chang Yung Fa and Co Kim Cham v. Hon. Emilio L. Gianzo to mean and apply strictly in accordance with Verba legis non est recedendum, holding that Yes. The law commands that an overstaying foreigner 'shall be deported'. An alien has no constitutional right to remain in a foreign country once his visa expir.
* Principal Reasoning: When you enter a country on a tourist visa, you promise to leave when your time is up. The statute says overstaying aliens 'shall be deported'. The word 'shall' gives the Immigration Commissioner a direct order to send you home. Judges cannot turn 'shall' into a permanent stay permit.

X. STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION OR JUDICIAL LEGISLATION?
[X] Proper statutory construction    [ ] Judicial legislation    [ ] Debatable

CASE #79: VICTORIAS MILLING COMPANY, INC. V. SOCIAL SECURITY COMMISSION
Citation: G.R. No. L-16704 | March 17, 1962 | 4 SCRA 627 | En Banc | Ponente: J. Barrera
===============================================================================================

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LAYER A: ALAC DIGEST, RECITATION SCRIPT & STATCON ANALYSIS
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

I. PARTIES & PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND
* Plaintiff/Petitioner: Victorias Milling Company, Inc. (Petitioner)
* Defendant/Respondent: Social Security Commission (Respondent)
* Originating Court: Social Security Commission / Supreme Court En Banc
* Nature of the Action: Petition for Review of Social Security Commission Resolution requiring the inclusion of bonuses, overtime pay, and commissions in the statutory definition of 'Compensation' under Republic Act No. 1161 (Social Security Act).

II. WHOLE FACTS
The Social Security Commission issued Circular No. 22 directing all employers that under Section 8(f) of Republic Act No. 1161, as amended by Republic Act No. 1792, the term 'Compensation' includes 'all remunerations for employment, including bonuses, overtime pay, and cash allowances,' and ordering the inclusion of these items in computing monthly SSS premium contributions. Victorias Milling Company protested, arguing that in the original 1954 SSS Act, Section 8(f) explicitly excluded bonuses, overtime, and allowances. When Congress amended the section in 1957 by deleting the exclusion phrase, Victorias argued that the deletion did not mean Congress intended to include bonuses, and that 'compensation' should be strictly interpreted as basic wage only.

Plain Meaning Summary of Facts:
The Social Security Commission ordered companies to pay SSS contributions based on total employee compensation, including overtime pay and bonuses. A sugar milling company objected, pointing out that in the original SSS law, bonuses and overtime were specifically excluded from the definition of 'compensation'. However, Congress later amended the law and deliberately DELETED the exclusion words. The Supreme Court ruled that when Congress deletes an exclusion, it intentionally broadens the statutory term 'compensation' to cover all earnings.

III. THE LEGAL ISSUE
Whether or not the statutory term 'Compensation' in Section 8(f) of the amended SSS Act includes bonuses, overtime pay, and allowances following the legislative deletion of the exclusion clause.

[HOW TO TACKLE IN RECITATION: THE LEGAL ISSUE]
* Plain Meaning: Does 'compensation' under the SSS law include overtime pay and bonuses after Congress deleted the clause that used to exclude them?
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the issue is whether the legislative deletion of an exclusion clause expands the statutory definition of 'Compensation' under Section 8(f) of RA 1161, testing Legislative History and Amendatory Deletions as Intrinsic/Extrinsic Aids."

IV. ALAC DIGEST

[A] ANSWER:
YES. 'Compensation' INCLUDES bonuses, overtime pay, and allowances. The deliberate LEGISLATIVE DELETION of the exclusion clause in the amendatory statute (R.A. No. 1792) conclusively demonstrates the legislative intent to make the definition of compensation all-inclusive.

* Plain Meaning: Yes. When Congress strikes out an exclusion from a law, it means the excluded items are now INCLUDED. 'Compensation' means everything an employee receives for working, so SSS contributions must be based on total pay.
* Recitation Script: "Yes, Sir/Ma'am. The Supreme Court held that under Legislative History and the effect of Amendatory Deletions, when Congress amended Section 8(f) of RA 1161 and deleted the express exclusion of bonuses and overtime, the inescapable legislative intent was to embrace ALL employee remunerations within the statutory term 'Compensation'."

[L] LEGAL BASIS:
* 1. Section 8(f), Republic Act No. 1161 (as amended by R.A. No. 1792): Defines 'Compensation' as all remuneration for employment, including the cash value of any remuneration paid in any medium other than cash.
* 2. Legislative Deletion as an Aid to Statutory Construction: The deletion of a limiting or excluding phrase in an amendatory statute is conclusive proof of legislative intent to remove the limitation and broaden the term.
* 3. Liberal Construction of Social Welfare Legislation: Social security statutes are construed liberally in favor of expanding worker benefits and system sustainability.
* Plain Meaning: Section 8(f) RA 1161 as amended, Effect of Legislative Deletions, and Liberal Social Construction.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the legal basis is Section 8(f) of RA 1161 as amended by RA 1792, applied under the rule on the Effect of Statutory Amendatory Deletions and liberal construction of social legislation."

[A] ANALYSIS / APPLICATION:
* 1. Significance of Amendatory Deletions: Under the original 1954 law, Section 8(f) ended with 'excluding overtime pay, bonuses, and allowances.' In 1957, Congress deliberately repealed that phrase. In statutory construction, deleting an exception abolishes the exception and restores the universal scope of the term.
* 2. All-Inclusive Definition of Compensation: The term 'all remuneration for employment' is comprehensive. Overtime pay and performance bonuses are earned directly from employment and constitute compensation.
* 3. Advancement of Social Security Purpose: Expanding the compensation base ensures higher pension benefits for retiring workers and guarantees the actuarial solvency of the social security fund.
* Plain Meaning: If a law says 'fruits, excluding apples', and later Congress deletes the words 'excluding apples', it is obvious that apples are now included! By deleting the exclusion for overtime and bonuses, Congress intentionally expanded 'compensation' to include every peso earned by the worker.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the Court applied the canon on amendatory deletions. Deleting an express exclusion indicates a clear legislative intent to embrace what was previously excluded. Under liberal construction of social legislation, 'Compensation' in the amended SSS Act includes all overtime, bonuses, and allowances."

[C] CONCLUSION:
The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the resolution of the Social Security Commission, HOLDING that bonuses, overtime pay, and allowances form part of 'Compensation' under Section 8(f) of RA 1161.

* Plain Meaning: The sugar milling company lost and SSS contributions were ordered computed on total employee pay including bonuses and overtime.
* Recitation Script: "The Supreme Court affirmed the SSC ruling, holding that legislative deletion of an exclusion expands the statutory term 'Compensation' to all employee earnings."

V. RELATION TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Syllabus Topic Alignment:
  • Chapter V: Interpretation of Words and Phrases
  - Effect of Amendatory Deletions & Broad Construction of 'Compensation' in Social Legislation

* Governing Latin Maxims with English Translations:
  1. Expressio unius est exclusio alterius (the express mention of one thing is the exclusion of another)
     - Literal Translation: The express mention of one thing implies the exclusion of others.
     - StatCon Application: The deletion of the exclusionary phrase eliminated the statutory exclusion entirely.
  2. Optima statuti interpretatrix est ipsum statutum (the best interpreter of a statute is the statute itself)
     - Literal Translation: The best interpreter of a statute is the statute itself.
     - StatCon Application: Comparing the original text with the amendatory act reveals the definitive legislative intent.
  3. Salus populi est suprema lex (the welfare of the people is the supreme law)
     - Literal Translation: The welfare of the people is the supreme law.
     - StatCon Application: Maximizing retirement and social security protections for the working class advances public welfare.

* Case Violation in StatCon: The employer attempted to revive a repealed statutory exception that Congress had deliberately deleted from the amendatory statute.
* Doctrinal Execution: Statutory Effect of Legislative Deletions: When the legislature amends a statute by deliberately deleting a restrictive or exclusionary clause, the deletion is conclusive proof of legislative intent to broaden the operative term to its unrestricted natural scope.

* Plain StatCon Meaning: When Congress deletes a line from a law that used to say 'except this', it means that thing is now included under the law.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, Victorias Milling v. SSC is the leading case on the effect of statutory deletions: deleting an exclusion broadens the statutory term 'Compensation' under Optima statuti interpretatrix."

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LAYER B: STANDARDIZED CASE DIGEST (STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION FORMAT)

* A. Situs of Statutory Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Supreme Court of the Philippines, exercising its constitutional judicial power under Article VIII of the Constitution, serves as the ultimate situs and final arbiter for interpreting and construing the disputed provision.
* B. Purpose of Statutory Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The purpose of construction is to discover and effectuate the true legislative intent and will of the lawmaking body, preventing injustice and ensuring the statute operates harmoniously.
* C. When to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. Construction became imperative because the text contained an ambiguity, competing interpretations, or conflicting statutory provisions that required judicial determination of legislative intent.
* D. Ambiguity or Vagueness - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The controversy centered on the ambiguity, silence, or conflicting scope of the statutory language, which the Court resolved by examining legislative intent and governing maxims.
* E. Verba Legis Non Est Recedendum - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The Court looked beyond literal words to discover the underlying legislative spirit, context, and intent.
* F. Absoluta Sententia Expositore Non Indigent - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The statutory language required expository interpretation due to textual gaps, broad phrasing, or conflicting provisions.
* G. Legislative Intent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court analyzed the true intent and policy of the lawmaking authority, ensuring that the statutory purpose is preserved and not defeated by erroneous administrative or private interpretations.
* H. Strict or Liberal Construction - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The Court applied standard ordinary rules of statutory interpretation without resorting to special strict or liberal construction doctrines.
* I. Prospective or Retrospective Construction - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The case involved only the contemporaneous, prospective application of the governing law.
* J. Statutory Construction vs. Judicial Legislation - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court underscored the constitutional boundary of judicial power: Courts are empowered solely to interpret (jus dicere), not to enact or amend laws (jus dare). Supplying unwritten terms or expanding clear prohibitions constitutes prohibited judicial legislation.
* K. Legis Interpretatio Legis Vim Obtinet - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. Under Article 8 of the Civil Code, the Supreme Court's authoritative interpretation of the statute forms part of the legal system of the Philippines and acquires the force of law (*legis interpretatio legis vim obtinet*).
* L. Extent and Limits of the Power to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court respected the constitutional limits of its authority by applying the law as written and refusing to invade the legislative domain under the guise of equity or expediency.

VIII. OTHER CANONS OR METHODS OF CONSTRUCTION
* Canon/Method: • Expressio unius est exclusio alterius (elimination of exceptions)
• Salus populi est suprema lex (social security expansion)
* How Applied: Applied as corroborative canons to reinforce the primary rule of construction.
* Effect on Interpretation: Harmonized the statute and prevented an absurd or unjust result.

IX. THE CONSTRUCTION MADE BY THE COURT
* Summary Formula: The Court construed the disputed statutory provisions in Victorias Milling Company, Inc. v. Social Security Commission to mean and apply strictly in accordance with Optima statuti interpretatrix est ipsum statutum, holding that Yes. When Congress strikes out an exclusion from a law, it means the excluded items are now INCLUDED. 'Compensation' means everything an employee receives for w.
* Principal Reasoning: If a law says 'fruits, excluding apples', and later Congress deletes the words 'excluding apples', it is obvious that apples are now included! By deleting the exclusion for overtime and bonuses, Congress intentionally expanded 'compensation' to include every peso earned by the worker.

X. STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION OR JUDICIAL LEGISLATION?
[X] Proper statutory construction    [ ] Judicial legislation    [ ] Debatable
Explanation: The Supreme Court acted within the legitimate bounds of its constitutional power to interpret the law (jus dicere). By applying Optima statuti interpretatrix est ipsum statutum, the Court faithfully effectuated the legislative intent without creating new rules, modifying clear statutory text, or usurping congressional prerogative. The ruling reinforces the separation of powers and the doctrine that courts must enforce the law as enacted.

XI. DOCTRINE:
Statutory Effect of Legislative Deletions: When the legislature amends a statute by deliberately deleting a restrictive or exclusionary clause, the deletion is conclusive proof of legislative intent to broaden the operative term to its unrestricted natural scope.

XII. ONE-SENTENCE TAKEAWAY:
This case is important in Statutory Construction because When Congress deletes a line from a law that used to say 'except this', it means that thing is now included under the law.

===============================================================================================
CASE #80: COMMISSIONER OF INTERNAL REVENUE V. MANILA LODGE NO. 761, B.P.O.E.
Citation: G.R. No. L-11176 | June 29, 1959 | 105 Phil. 938 | En Banc | Ponente: J. Bautista Angelo
===============================================================================================

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LAYER A: ALAC DIGEST, RECITATION SCRIPT & STATCON ANALYSIS
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

I. PARTIES & PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND
* Plaintiff/Petitioner: Commissioner of Internal Revenue (Petitioner)
* Defendant/Respondent: Manila Lodge No. 761, Benevolent and Protective Order of Elks (Respondent)
* Originating Court: Court of Tax Appeals / Supreme Court En Banc
* Nature of the Action: Petition for Review of CTA decision exempting a fraternal club from fixed and percentage taxes on restaurant and bar operations under Section 191 of the National Internal Revenue Code.

II. WHOLE FACTS
Manila Lodge No. 761 of the Benevolent and Protective Order of Elks (BPOE) is a non-profit fraternal organization that maintains a clubhouse in Manila with a dining room and bar operated exclusively for its members and their invited guests. The Collector of Internal Revenue assessed Manila Lodge for percentage taxes as a 'keeper of restaurant, refreshment parlor, and bar' under Section 191 of the Tax Code. Manila Lodge protested, arguing that in statutory construction, the words 'business', 'restaurant', and 'bar' in tax statutes apply strictly to commercial enterprises operated FOR PROFIT and open to the GENERAL PUBLIC.

Plain Meaning Summary of Facts:
The Elks Club is a private fraternal club that runs an exclusive dining room and bar solely for its members, with no profit motive and closed to the general public. The BIR tried to tax the club as a commercial restaurant and bar under the Tax Code. The Supreme Court ruled in favor of the club, holding that in tax statutes, terms like 'restaurant', 'bar', and 'business' imply commercial enterprises open to the public for profit, not exclusive private clubhouses.

III. THE LEGAL ISSUE
Whether or not a private, non-profit fraternal club operating a dining room and bar exclusively for members is a 'keeper of restaurant or bar' engaged in business under Section 191 of the Tax Code.

[HOW TO TACKLE IN RECITATION: THE LEGAL ISSUE]
* Plain Meaning: Is an exclusive private members' club subject to commercial restaurant taxes when it serves food to members without a profit motive?
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the issue is whether a non-profit fraternal clubhouse falls within the statutory meaning of 'restaurant' and 'business' under Section 191 NIRC, testing the Construction of Commercial Terms (Strictissimi Juris in Taxation)."

IV. ALAC DIGEST

[A] ANSWER:
NO. Manila Lodge is NOT a commercial restaurant or bar and is NOT subject to the tax. In statutory construction of tax codes, terms like 'business' and 'restaurant' denote commercial activities conducted FOR PROFIT and catering to the GENERAL PUBLIC.

* Plain Meaning: No. In tax law, words like 'restaurant' and 'bar' mean public commercial businesses run to make money. A private club serving food only to its members at cost is not a public commercial business.
* Recitation Script: "No, Sir/Ma'am. The Supreme Court held that under Strictissimi Juris in tax statutes, the terms 'business', 'restaurant', and 'bar' denote commercial enterprises open to the general public for pecuniary profit. A non-profit fraternal club catering exclusively to members is not engaged in commercial business under Section 191 NIRC."

[L] LEGAL BASIS:
* 1. Section 191, National Internal Revenue Code: Levies percentage taxes on keepers of restaurants, bars, and refreshment parlors engaged in business.
* 2. Statutory Definition of 'Business' and 'Commercial Terms': In taxation, 'business' means that which occupies the time, attention, and labor of men for the purpose of a livelihood or profit.
* 3. Strictissimi Juris (of the strictest interpretation) in Tax Imposition: Tax burdens are never presumed and cannot be extended to non-commercial, private fraternal entities.
* Plain Meaning: Section 191 NIRC, Definition of 'Business', and Strictissimi Juris.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the legal basis is Section 191 NIRC, interpreted under the commercial definition of 'business' and the canon Strictissimi Juris in taxation."

[A] ANALYSIS / APPLICATION:
* 1. Ordinary and Technical Meaning of 'Business': The word 'business' in tax jurisprudence denotes an activity undertaken for commercial gain or profit. The Elks Club operated its canteen on a non-profit basis purely for member convenience.
* 2. The Element of Public Catering: A 'restaurant' or 'bar' under Section 191 implies an establishment holding itself out to the general public. An exclusive fraternal facility restricted strictly to members is not a public restaurant.
* 3. Strict Construction Against Taxing Authority: Taxes are burdens that must be explicitly authorized. The BIR cannot stretch commercial categories to tax private social and fraternal arrangements.
* Plain Meaning: The Tax Code taxes businesses that make profits by selling meals to the public. The Elks Club is a private club that buys food for its own members at cost. It does not sell to the public and makes zero profit. Because tax terms must be given their commercial meaning, the club is not a 'restaurant business'.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the Court applied the commercial definition of statutory words and Strictissimi Juris. The terms 'restaurant' and 'business' in the Tax Code require a profit motive and public service. The Court held that exclusive fraternal canteens are exempt from percentage business taxes."

[C] CONCLUSION:
The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the decision of the Court of Tax Appeals, CANCELLING the tax assessments against Manila Lodge No. 761.

* Plain Meaning: The Elks Club won and the commercial restaurant tax assessment was cancelled.
* Recitation Script: "The Supreme Court cancelled the tax assessment, holding that private non-profit fraternal clubs do not constitute commercial 'restaurants' under Section 191 NIRC."

V. RELATION TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Syllabus Topic Alignment:
  • Chapter V: Interpretation of Words and Phrases
  - Commercial and Technical Words: The Meaning of 'Business' and 'Restaurant' in Tax Jurisprudence (Strictissimi Juris)

* Governing Latin Maxims with English Translations:
  1. Strictissimi juris (of the strictest interpretation)
     - Literal Translation: Of the strictest law.
     - StatCon Application: Tax statutes imposing burdens on businesses are construed strictly against the government.
  2. Verba legis non est recedendum (from the words of the statute there should be no departure)
     - Literal Translation: From the words of the statute there must be no departure.
     - StatCon Application: Commercial words in tax codes must be given their standard commercial meaning.
  3. Noscitur a sociis (a thing is known by its associates)
     - Literal Translation: It is known by its companions.
     - StatCon Application: The term 'keepers of restaurants' is colored by associated commercial business establishments in Section 191.

* Case Violation in StatCon: The BIR attempted to stretch the commercial terms 'restaurant' and 'business' to encompass private, non-profit fraternal clubs.
* Doctrinal Execution: Commercial Construction of Tax Words: Statutory words designating commercial entities ('business', 'restaurant') are construed in their ordinary commercial sense requiring a profit motive and public trade; they cannot be extended to private non-profit arrangements.

* Plain StatCon Meaning: Words in tax laws like 'restaurant' and 'business' only apply to money-making businesses open to the public, not private clubs sharing meals among members.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, CIR v. Manila Lodge No. 761 is the classic authority establishing that commercial terms in tax statutes require a profit motive and public catering under Strictissimi Juris."

LAYER B: STANDARDIZED CASE DIGEST (STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION FORMAT)

Case Title: Commissioner of Internal Revenue v. Manila Lodge No. 761, B.P.O.E.
G.R. No.:   G.R. No. L-11176
Date:       June 29, 1959
Ponente:    En Banc

I. FACTS OF THE CASE
Manila Lodge No. 761 of the Benevolent and Protective Order of Elks (BPOE) is a non-profit fraternal organization that maintains a clubhouse in Manila with a dining room and bar operated exclusively for its members and their invited guests. The Collector of Internal Revenue assessed Manila Lodge for percentage taxes as a 'keeper of restaurant, refreshment parlor, and bar' under Section 191 of the Tax Code. Manila Lodge protested, arguing that in statutory construction, the words 'business', 'restaurant', and 'bar' in tax statutes apply strictly to commercial enterprises operated FOR PROFIT and open to the GENERAL PUBLIC.

II. FACTS RELEVANT TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Provision Relevant: 1. Section 191, National Internal Revenue Code: Levies percentage taxes on keepers of restaurants, bars, and refreshment parlors engaged in business.
2. Statutory Definition of 'Business' and 'Commercial Terms': In taxation, 'business' means that which occupies the time, attention, and labor of men for the purpose of a livelihood or profit.
* Circumstance Making Construction Necessary: The BIR attempted to stretch the commercial terms 'restaurant' and 'business' to encompass private, non-profit fraternal clubs.
* Competing Interpretations: Petitioner argued for a favorable interpretation based on statutory intent, while Respondent contended for a literal or restrictive application.

III. ISSUES
A. General Legal Issues: Whether or not a private, non-profit fraternal club operating a dining room and bar exclusively for members is a 'keeper of restaurant or bar' engaged in business under Section 191 of the Tax Code.
B. Statutory-Construction Issue/s: Whether the subject statutory act, classification, or terminology under the governing statute/rule means, includes, or applies to the factual controversy in Commissioner of Internal Revenue v. Manila Lodge No. 761, B.P.O.E., under the canon of Strictissimi juris.

IV. PROVISION/S OF LAW CONSTRUED
* Provision: 1. Section 191, National Internal Revenue Code: Levies percentage taxes on keepers of restaurants, bars, and refreshment parlors engaged in business.
2. Statutory Definition of 'Business' and 'Commercial Terms': In taxation, 'business' means that which occupies the time, attention, and labor of men for the purpose of a livelihood or profit.
* Interpretive Problem: Determining the precise legislative intent and boundary of the statutory terms in light of: The BIR attempted to stretch the commercial terms 'restaurant' and 'business' to encompass private, non-profit fraternal...

V. RULING OF THE SUPREME COURT
A. General Ruling: The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the decision of the Court of Tax Appeals, CANCELLING the tax assessments against Manila Lodge No. 761.
B. Ruling on Statutory-Construction Issue: NO. Manila Lodge is NOT a commercial restaurant or bar and is NOT subject to the tax. In statutory construction of tax codes, terms like 'business' and 'restaurant' denote commercial activities conducted FOR PROFIT and catering to the GENERAL PUBLIC.

VI. HOW THE COURT CONSTRUED THE LAW
A. Why Was Construction Necessary? Ambiguity in the application and scope of the statute: The BIR attempted to stretch the commercial terms 'restaurant' and 'business' to encompass private, non-profit fraternal clubs..
B. Purpose of Construction: To ascertain and give full effect to the true intent, spirit, and purpose of the lawmaking body.
C. Method/Canon Applied: Strictissimi juris
   - How Applied: Commercial Construction of Tax Words: Statutory words designating commercial entities ('business', 'restaurant') are construed in their ordinary commercial sense requiring a profit motive and public trade; they cannot be extended to private non-profit arrangements.
   - Result: No. In tax law, words like 'restaurant' and 'bar' mean public commercial businesses run to make money. A private club serving food only to its members at cost is not a public commercial business.
D. Other Sources Used: Ordinary meaning of words, statute considered as a whole, legislative history, and established Supreme Court jurisprudence.

VII. RELEVANT STATUTORY-CONSTRUCTION CONCEPTS (A–L EVALUATION)
* A. Situs of Statutory Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Supreme Court of the Philippines, exercising its constitutional judicial power under Article VIII of the Constitution, serves as the ultimate situs and final arbiter for interpreting and construing the disputed provision.
* B. Purpose of Statutory Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The purpose of construction is to discover and effectuate the true legislative intent and will of the lawmaking body, preventing injustice and ensuring the statute operates harmoniously.
* C. When to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court reaffirmed the cardinal threshold rule: Construction and interpretation are proper only where the statute is ambiguous, doubtful, or susceptible of multiple meanings. Where the law is clear, no construction is warranted.
* D. Ambiguity or Vagueness - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The statutory language was clear and unambiguous; the Court declined to find ambiguity where none existed in the text.
* E. Verba Legis Non Est Recedendum - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court adhered strictly to the literal wording of the statute, holding that from the words of a statute there should be no departure.
* F. Absoluta Sententia Expositore Non Indigent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court held that when the language of the law is clear and unequivocal, it requires no interpreter and leaves no room for judicial construction.
* G. Legislative Intent - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court analyzed the true intent and policy of the lawmaking authority, ensuring that the statutory purpose is preserved and not defeated by erroneous administrative or private interpretations.
* H. Strict or Liberal Construction - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court applied the recognized standard of construction tailored to the nature of the statute (e.g., penal and tax statutes are strictly construed against the State, whereas social legislation and remedial rules are liberally construed).
* I. Prospective or Retrospective Construction - [NOT APPLICABLE]: Not Applicable. The case involved only the contemporaneous, prospective application of the governing law.
* J. Statutory Construction vs. Judicial Legislation - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court underscored the constitutional boundary of judicial power: Courts are empowered solely to interpret (jus dicere), not to enact or amend laws (jus dare). Supplying unwritten terms or expanding clear prohibitions constitutes prohibited judicial legislation.
* K. Legis Interpretatio Legis Vim Obtinet - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. Under Article 8 of the Civil Code, the Supreme Court's authoritative interpretation of the statute forms part of the legal system of the Philippines and acquires the force of law (*legis interpretatio legis vim obtinet*).
* L. Extent and Limits of the Power to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court respected the constitutional limits of its authority by applying the law as written and refusing to invade the legislative domain under the guise of equity or expediency.

VIII. OTHER CANONS OR METHODS OF CONSTRUCTION
* Canon/Method: • Noscitur a sociis (commercial words defined by context)
• Verba legis non est recedendum (ordinary meaning of business terms)
* How Applied: Applied as corroborative canons to reinforce the primary rule of construction.
* Effect on Interpretation: Harmonized the statute and prevented an absurd or unjust result.

IX. THE CONSTRUCTION MADE BY THE COURT
* Summary Formula: The Court construed the disputed statutory provisions in Commissioner of Internal Revenue v. Manila Lodge No. 761, B.P.O.E. to mean and apply strictly in accordance with Strictissimi juris, holding that No. In tax law, words like 'restaurant' and 'bar' mean public commercial businesses run to make money. A private club serving food only to its members at cost i.
